기록상 `mcp-server` capability의 **범위 밖**입니다.

- 현재 `docs/ontology/capabilities/mcp-server.md`의 `Inclusions / Exclusions`는 MCP 도구 등록·I/O, Vault parser/writer, ontology graph query는 포함하지만, **`AST/source search engine`은 명시적으로 제외**합니다.
- 기존 `focusPath`도 파일 단위 static import 증거까지만 다루며, `symbol coupling`, affected behavior, runtime impact는 주장하지 않는다고 기록돼 있습니다. [DECISIONS.md](/Users/stark/orca/workspaces/oh-my-ontology/main-2/docs/DECISIONS.md:7051)
- 그 범위가 부족할 때의 다음 선택도 `mcp-server` 확장이 아니라 **별도의 `symbol/CodeGraph integration boundary` 설계**로 기록돼 있습니다. [DECISIONS.md](/Users/stark/orca/workspaces/oh-my-ontology/main-2/docs/DECISIONS.md:7064)
- 제품 경계 역시 definitions, callers, imports 같은 구조 검색은 CodeGraph·AST·source-search 계층에 두고, Atlas는 의미·승인·구현 증거·검증 경로를 담당한다고 명시합니다. [PRODUCT-DIRECTION.md](/Users/stark/orca/workspaces/oh-my-ontology/main-2/docs/PRODUCT-DIRECTION.md:104), [AGENT-MEMORY-POSITIONING.md](/Users/stark/orca/workspaces/oh-my-ontology/main-2/docs/AGENT-MEMORY-POSITIONING.md:34)

따라서 기록된 경계는 다음과 같습니다: **MCP transport와 tool registry는 `mcp-server` 안, 코드 심볼 검색 엔진의 책임은 별도 경계 밖**입니다. 다만 그 별도 capability의 이름이나 관계는 아직 확정 기록이 없고, “별도 symbol/CodeGraph integration boundary를 설계한다”까지만 기록돼 있습니다.

붙이는 것이 정상 시나리오이고, 배타적 단일선택 위젯은 **그 기능을 숨긴다**. 위계석의 목표(넷이 동등하게 읽힌다)는 **채움 해제만으로 달성**되므로 세그먼트는 목표에 불필요하다. 기능 은폐는 되돌릴 수 없는 손실이라 형태 논거가 이를 이기지 못한다.", "+", "+2. **감지 신호를 지금 배선할 것인가.** 작업대석 「이미 있는 파일 존재 신호로 자신도 같은 위험을 반대 논점으로 적었다 — 파일 존재는 「지금 쓰는 도구」가 아니라 **「예전에 한 번 쓴 도구」**를 가리킬 수 있다.", "+", "+3. **카드 크롬을 이번에 걷을 것인가.** 위계석 「미루자」 vs 체계석 「동반 필수」 — **충돌이 아니었다.** 재보니 서로 다른 크기를 말하고 있었다: 위계석이 미룬 것은 **카피 통합**(두 Tail-only references (`mcp-server` for `capabilities/mcp-server`) are also redirected. Without `confirm:true`, runs as a dry-run with a full update preview; each `backlinkUpdates.updates[]` row includes the referrer `slug`, `title`, changed frontmatter keys, and `bodyChanged`. Key changes preserve relation evidence as a nonblank string, nonempty string array, or nonempty nonblank string map such as `relation_notes`; the internal atomic-write plan is never returned. Throws if `newSlug` already exists unless `overwrite:true` is passed. Replaces the manual loop of `find_backlinks` + N `patch_concept` calls. **R11**: optional `expected_mtime` for the source slug. 입력 칸은 최고빈도 표면이고 내 헌장이 그 예산을 `0~--motion-fast` 로 못박는다. `--motion-base`(180ms)는 내가 내 규칙을 어긴 것이다.\"*", "+**반증 조건**: 자람을 켠 뒤 **「타자가 무겁다」는 보고**가 나오면 반대가 옳았다. 그때 답은 임의로 깎는 것이 아니라 `--motion-fast`(120ms)로 **한 단 내리는 것**이고, 그래도 무거우면 전이를 제거한다. **소유자가 호소한 잘린 글자를 실제로 없애는 것은 자람이 아니라 스크롤 배수 정렬**이므로 이 반대가 이겨도 증상은 안 돌아온다.", "+", "+**2. 상호작용석 — 드롭다운을 뒤로 미루면.**", "+> *\"지금 안 고치면 사용자는 `gemma4:12b` 하나로 영원히 갇힌다.\"*", "+**반증 조건**: 채택됐으므로 이 반대는 이겼다. 반대 방향의 반증 — 포털 전환 후 다른 표면에서 팝오버 위치 회귀가 관측되면 프리미티브 전환이 성급했던 것이다.", "+", "+**3. 작업대석 — 웹 CORS 관측의 수명.**", "+> *\"「웹에서 Ollama 가 CORS 를 막지 않는다」는 이 기계의 이 순간의 기본값일 뿐이다. 선택했다」로 썼다** — 기본값이 바뀌어도 참인 문장이다. 웹 게이트를 여는 조건은 **po-steward 판정 대기**(질문 3, 미도착 — 도착 시 후속 기록).", "+", "+**4. 지킴이 — 코드 존재가 동작 증명은 아니다.**", "+> *\"쓰기 파이프라인이 코드상 있다고 해서 사용자 경험상 작동하는 건 아니다. 코드 존재만으로 「신설 ./docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r3.txt:884:docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r2.txt-205-docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-B1-on.txt:1728:./src/entities/docs-vault/data/manifest.headings.json:10956:      "slug": "3-mcp-server-current-runtime-inventory"
./docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r3.txt:885:docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r2.txt-206-docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-B1-on.txt:1841:./src/entities/docs-vault/data/content.json:2:  "AGENT-GRAPH-WORKFLOW": "# Agent Graph Workflow\n\n> Current as of 2026-08-02. This is the user-facing guide for running\n> `ontology-atlas` as a local meaning graph: CLI-only, MCP-connected, and web\n> workbench flows over the same markdown vault.\n\n`ontology-atlas` is not a hosted graph database. It is a local-first graph\nworkbench where markdown frontmatter is the graph, git is the audit log, and AI\nagents can read or write through MCP when they are connected.\n\n> **How the server reaches you (decided 2026-07-27, `docs/DECISIONS.md`):**\n> npm publishing is retired. There is no `ontology-atlas` or\n> `ontology-atlas-mcp` package to install, and there never will be. Two live\n> paths instead:\n>\n> - **Installed app** — open the vault folder and press **Connect agent**. The app carries a compiled MCP server in its own bundle,\n>   shows you the config it is about to write, writes it, then spawns the\n>   server and round-trips `get_concept` before reporting success.\n> - **Source checkout** — register the server as\n>   `node /absolute/path/to/ontology-atlas/mcp/src/index.js` and run CLI proofs\n>   as `node cli/src/index.mjs <command>`.\n>\n> A config still holding `command: \"npx\"` predates the bundled server and\n> cannot start.\n>\n> Command examples below are written as `ontology-atlas <command>` for\n> readability — that is the CLI's name, not a global binary. From a source\n> checkout, run each as `node cli/src/index.mjs <command>`.\n\n## Official Client Contract\n\nChecked against official docs on 2026-06-04:\n\n- Codex supports MCP servers in the CLI and IDE extension, stores MCP server\n  configuration in Codex `config.toml`, and exposes `codex mcp list` plus the\n  `/mcp` TUI panel for checking active servers.\n  Project-scoped `.codex/config.toml` is ignored until Codex marks that project\n  trusted; `codex mcp list` from the trusted folder and `connection_info` are\n  therefore required connection evidence, not optional diagnostics.\n  Source: https://developers.openai.com/codex/mcp\n- Claude Code configures MCP servers with `claude mcp`, checks connected\n  servers with `claude mcp list` and `/mcp`, and supports local stdio servers\n  for tools that need direct system access.\n  Source: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/mcp\n\nAtlas keeps the Codex boundary machine-readable too: `agent-setup --json`\nreports `clientStatus.codex.projectTrust: \"unknown\"` and\n`registration: \"unverified\"` until a fresh Codex session passes the trust\nprompt, `codex mcp list`, and Atlas `connection_info`. If the project config is\nignored, use the generated global `codex mcp add ...` fallback, restart Codex,\nand repeat those checks. Atlas cannot inspect Codex credentials or trust\ndirectly, so config readiness never becomes a live-connection claim.\n\nOntology Atlas therefore does not reimplement Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor chat\ninside the app — it does not own an agent loop, model routing, API keys, or\nbilling. It prepares the local MCP files, root-specific commands, restart\nguidance, and verification gates so those agents can connect from their own app,\nterminal, or IDE session and work against the same vault.\n\n**Reversal record, 2026-07-26 — the embedded terminal is gone.** Earlier that\nsame day this section said the desktop app *may host a terminal*, and a bottom\ndock shipped that ran your already-installed `claude` / `codex` CLI. The owner\nreversed it within the week, and the reasoning is kept here rather than deleted.\n\nWhy it was removed: the dock's own trust contract made it a strict subset of the\nterminal you already use — one login shell with no arguments, no tabs, no\nsplits, no shell profiles, the session ending when you collapsed it, and about\nthirteen visible rows on a 14-inch screen. Every one of those was the right\ncall, and together they meant long agent loops, resumed sessions, and parallel\nworktrees belonged somewhere else. The synergy that seemed to justify embedding\n— *the agent edits the vault and the map reacts immediately* — turned out to be\nposition-independent: the vault watcher observes the folder on disk, so an agent\nrunning in iTerm moves the map exactly the same way. Polishing the window did\nnot change the comparison, and sunk cost is not a reason to keep a surface.\n\n**Atlas does not host a terminal; it hands off to yours.** The bridge between a\nperson and an agent is a protocol, not a window: the MCP tools, the CLI, the\n`agent-brief` handoff prompt, and the vault watcher. The vault agent panel gives\nyou a copy block — `cd <your vault>` plus the sentence to paste — and you run it\nwhere your session already lives. What you change out there comes back through\nthe watcher and the recent-change lens on the map.\n\n**Nothing runs on its own.** Atlas never issues a command, spawns a process, or\nstarts an agent on your behalf. It prepares files and text you choose to run.\n\n## Choose The Right Mode\n\nUse this table before setup. The modes share the same vault files, so switching\nbetween them does not migrate data.\n\n| Situation | Start here | What you get |\n|---|---|---|\n| You only want to inspect a local vault from Terminal | CLI-only | Validation, workspace summaries, graph scans, path/explain queries, and graph DB packs without any MCP client |\n| Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor is connected to MCP | MCP-connected | The agent can call read/write tools directly, receive structured repair fields, and update the markdown vault after validation |\n| You want graph-database-style exploration but not a database server | Graph DB pack | Bounded query plans, node/edge scans, domain matrix, paths, relation explanations, and follow-up evidence commands |\n| Setup is unclear or you opened the agent from another codebase root | Agent setup gate | Config repair commands, restart guidance, JSON readiness checks, and fallback timing before edits |\n\nThe installed app *does* claim a one-click agent connection, because it carries\nthe server it is connecting you to. Press **Connect agent**: the\napp shows the config it is about to write, writes it after you approve, then\nspawns the bundled binary and round-trips `get_concept` — a green light means\nyour vault is readable, not that a process started. Source-checkout contributors\nregister the local MCP entry point instead, restart their agent, run the JSON\ngate, and only then ask the agent to write ontology updates. A browser surface\nhas no absolute path to write, so it stays honestly demoted to the\nsource-checkout instructions rather than emitting a config that cannot boot.\n\nRead the JSON gate in three states:\n\n- `ok: false` means setup or fallback command execution is broken. Fix the\n  config before asking the agent to edit ontology files.\n- `ok: true` with `performanceOk: false` means the local graph path works, but\n  fallback latency is slow enough to inspect before relying on it heavily.\n- `ok: true` with `performanceOk: true` means the setup and fallback graph path\n  are ready for read-first agent work.\n\nFor humans, `agent-brief --verify-fallbacks` prints the same setup gate summary\nbefore the row list: `ok=true performanceOk=true wall=... slow=0/N failed=0`.\nThat line is the fastest way to tell whether a connector-less Claude Code/Codex\nsession can trust the local CLI graph path before scanning or writing.\n\n## What Works Without MCP Connected\n\nYou can use the product without connecting Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, or any\nMCP client.\n\nThe CLI currently exposes 54 commands over the same local vault, including\ngraph-database-style queries:\n\n```bash\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs validate docs/ontology\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs workspace-brief docs/ontology\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs match-nodes docs/ontology --kind capability --limit 10\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs match-edges docs/ontology --type depends_on --limit 10\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs domain-matrix docs/ontology --types depends_on,relates\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs agent-brief docs/ontology --graph-db-pack\n```\n\nThis mode is useful when an agent has no MCP connector available. The CLI still\nuses the same local graph engine and the same vault files; it just prints the\nanswers in terminal form instead of returning JSON-RPC tool results to an agent.\n\nUse `agent-setup` when the vault already exists and you only want to repair\nagent config files:\n\n```bash\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs agent-setup /absolute/path/to/vault --root /absolute/path/to/codebase --write\n```\n\nThat command creates missing `.mcp.json` and `.codex/config.toml` files without\nadding starter markdown. For a parseable existing file, it atomically merges or\nrebinds only the `ontology-atlas` JSON entry / TOML section pair and preserves\nunrelated servers, sections, and comments. Invalid or duplicate Atlas config is\nleft untouched with a merge template and a nonzero review result.\nIts terminal and JSON output also point back to this guide\n(`docs/AGENT-GRAPH-WORKFLOW.md`), so CLI-only setup logs still tell a human\nwhere to read the MCP, graph DB, and verification differences.\nThe same guide path is included in `agent-brief --prompt` and\n`agent-brief --graph-db-pack`, so the agent handoff and connector-less graph\nquery script carry the explanation forward after setup. The normal\n`agent-brief` terminal view and the shell-pasteable `--graph-db-pack` header now\nalso render the same mode guide directly, so a human can tell when to stay\nCLI-only, when MCP adds value, when to use the graph DB pack, and when to run the\nsetup gate without opening JSON first.\nThe `/ontology/insights` graph DB query pack card shows the same mode guide\nbefore its copy buttons, and the copied UI CLI pack includes the guide too, so\nthe explanation survives when a non-developer passes only the runbook into a\nfresh Claude Code or Codex session.\n`agent-setup --json` also includes `docs.modeComparison`, a machine-readable\nversion of the CLI-only / MCP-connected / graph DB pack / setup gate choice, so\nan AI agent can explain the right path without scraping this Markdown table.\nFor automation, `agent-brief --json` and MCP `query_ontology({operation:\n\"agent_brief\"})` expose the same location as `docs.workflowGuide`, so an AI\ntool does not need to parse the human prompt to find the guide.\nThey also expose this page's mode chooser as `docs.modeComparison` and the\nscan-to-proof rules as `docs.graphScanProofChecklist`, so an AI tool can inspect\nthe CLI-only / MCP-connected / graph DB pack / setup gate choice and the\nrequired `totalMatches` / follow-up / `evidence.pathsComplete` steps without\nparsing Markdown.\n\n## What MCP Adds\n\nMCP is the agent interface. When Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor has the\n`ontology-atlas` MCP server registered, `tools/list` provides the exact current\nlocal read/write inventory. Read tools cover connection and git state, node and\nevidence lookup, graph queries, validation, compilation, and source analysis.\nWrite tools cover guarded concept/relation changes, source binding, local\nsnapshots, and project-meaning finalization. Run `mcp-verify` to prove that the\nadvertised set, initialize guidance, and this vault agree.\n\nMCP adds three things that terminal-only use does not provide as naturally:\n\n1. The agent can fetch precise context on demand instead of asking the user to\n   paste terminal output.\n2. The agent can write back to the same markdown vault after it has read,\n   validated, and preflighted the change.\n3. Tool responses include structured repair fields, result contracts, and\n   write guardrails so the agent can recover from bad inputs without guessing.\n\nThe first MCP calls should be read-only:\n\n```json\n{ \"tool\": \"validate_vault\", \"arguments\": {} }\n{ \"tool\": \"query_ontology\", \"arguments\": { \"operation\": \"workspace_brief\" } }\n{ \"tool\": \"query_ontology\", \"arguments\": { \"operation\": \"agent_brief\" } }\n{ \"tool\": \"query_ontology\", \"arguments\": { \"operation\": \"health\" } }\n```\n\nOnly after those checks are clean should an agent propose writes.\n\nAfter the person accepts project competency answers and the agent has completed\nthe related concept/relation writes, `validate_vault`, and a complete compile,\ncall `finalize_project_meaning({ projectSlug, expected_mtime })`. The server\nderives the body/graph/source provenance and writes only the receipt—never raw\nanswers, witness text, a private absolute source root, or remote coordinates.\n`ok: true` means that receipt write completed; it does not mean the project is\nverified. Read `agent_brief.meaningAssessment` for the categorical fail-closed\nstate (`verified_current`, `review_required`, `needs_evidence`, or `invalid`).\nFor a multi-project vault, use `query_ontology({ operation: \"agent_brief\",\nproject: \"SLUG\" })` or `ontology-atlas agent-brief <vault> --project SLUG`.\n\n## How This Differs From A Graph Database\n\n`ontology-atlas` deliberately borrows graph DB query habits, but optimizes for a\ndifferent job.\n\n| Need | Graph DB | ontology-atlas |\n|---|---|---|\n| Storage | Server/database files | Plain markdown files in your repo or local folder |\n| Setup | Database service, schema, credentials | Pick or create a folder; no login or backend |\n| Query language | Cypher/Gremlin/SPARQL style | CLI commands and MCP `query_ontology` operations |\n| Source of truth | Database state | Git-tracked markdown frontmatter |\n| Human readability | Usually requires UI/export | Every node is an editable `.md` document |\n| Agent use | Agent needs DB tooling and schema context | Agent gets MCP tools, first-call guidance, result contracts, and write guardrails |\n| Write safety | Transaction/schema constraints | dry-runs, `relation_check`, `expected_mtime`, validation, maintenance queues |\n| Best scale | Large transactional graphs | Codebase/team memory graphs that need explainable local context |\n\nSo the claim is not \"faster than every graph DB at every graph workload.\" The\nclaim is narrower and more useful for this product: for a developer's local\ncodebase memory, it is more practical because the graph is inspectable, editable,\ngit-reviewable, and directly available to AI coding agents.\n\n## Graph-DB-Style Query Pack\n\nFor agent or terminal sessions, start with a plan-first scan instead of pulling\nthe full graph:\n\n```bash\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs facets docs/ontology --limit 10\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs schema docs/ontology --limit 10\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs match-nodes docs/ontology --kind capability --limit 10\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs match-edges docs/ontology --type depends_on --limit 10\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs domain-matrix docs/ontology --types depends_on,relates\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs all-paths capabilities/cli-developer-entry capabilities/mcp-server docs/ontology --plan --force --max-hops 3 --types depends_on,relates\nnode $ATLAS/cli/src/index.mjs explain capabilities/cli-developer-entry capabilities/mcp-server docs/ontology --types depends_on,relates\n```\n\nImportant rule: scan rows are candidates, not proof. Before using a node or edge\nfor an onboarding, refactor, or write decision, follow up with `node`,\n`match-edges`, `blast-radius`, `explain`, or `relation-check`.\n\nUse this scan-to-proof checklist:\n\n1. Report `totalMatches`, `limited`, and returned row count from `match-nodes`\n   or `match-edges`.\n2. For a node row, run `node` / `node_profile` or `blast-radius` before using it\n   as onboarding or refactor evidence.\n3. For an edge row, run `explain`, `path`, and `relation-check` before using it\n   as coupling or write evidence.\n4. For path evidence, report `evidence.pathsComplete`; if it is false, narrow\n   the query before writing or making an architecture claim.\n\n## Actual Verification Snapshot\n\nThese checks were run against this repository's dogfood vault on 2026-07-27.\n\nCLI-only checks:\n\n- `node cli/src/index.mjs agent-setup docs/ontology --json`\n  - A cold-start or\nunqualified vault may still report `needs_attention`: `workspace_brief` and\n`agent_brief` must surface an invalid, unmeasured, or stale `meaningAssessment`\ninstead of relabelling it `healthy`/`ready`/`100`. The stronger `healthy` and\n`ready` result is reserved for a vault whose meaning assessment is current and\nwhose exact construction plan has passed the qualification and human-approval\ngate.\n\n## Recommended First User Flow\n\nFor a non-developer or a first-time AI-agent session:\n\n1. No CodeGraph, Serena, language\nserver, grep wrapper, or external source-index service is required for the\nproduct to be usable.\n\nAtlas should not promise to replace code-reading tools. Built-in source search,\ngrep, language servers, Serena, CodeGraph, AST indexes, and similar tools are\ngood at structural facts: definitions, callers, imports, routes, and local\nimpact paths. Atlas helps the coding agent one layer above that: it preserves\nthe task starting point, product meaning, capability boundary, implementation\nevidence, and validation path that explain why those code facts matter.\n\n## Product Bet\n\nThe product is not valuable because it is \"an ontology editor.\" Developers do\nnot want another system they must manually maintain.\n\nThe product is valuable if it reduces the maintenance cost of AI-agent memory:\n\n1. Open a repo and generate an ontology draft automatically.\n2. Let the agent propose mental-model changes after real code work.\n3. Let the developer review those changes like a git diff.\n4. Make the next agent session visibly better because the memory is already in\n   the repo.\n\nThat loop is the product. The ontology graph is the implementation substrate.\n\nThe coding-agent value is therefore not \"Atlas reads all code for you.\" The\nvalue is \"Atlas tells the agent what to inspect, why it matters, and what must\nbe verified before acting.\" Store meaningful implementation evidence as\n`element` nodes and relations; leave exhaustive symbol graphs to deterministic\ncode-intelligence tools.\n\n## Why This Can Matter\n\nThe strongest positioning is:\n\n> Your AI coding agent forgets your codebase. Give it a local, git-backed memory\n> it can read and maintain.\n\nThis is stronger than:\n\n> Local-first ontology graph workbench with deterministic compiler and MCP tools.\n\nThe second sentence is technically accurate, but it leads with implementation\nlanguage. The first sentence leads with the daily pain of AI-assisted coding.\n\n## Success Conditions\n\nontology-atlas becomes a real product if a new repo can show value in one short\nloop:\n\n```text\ninit -> bootstrap -> agent answers better through MCP -> agent proposes sync\n-> developer reviews diff -> next task benefits\n```\n\nTarget: the first visible value should appear within 10 minutes.\n\n## Failure Conditions\n\nThe product fails if users feel they must become ontology maintainers before\nthey get value.\n\nRisk signals:\n\n- onboarding starts with manual node authoring\n- the hosted website feels like the primary product instead of intro/download\n- agent sync is not visible in git diffs\n- `health`, `workspace_brief`, and `maintenance_plan` explain problems but do\n  not lead to obvious next actions\n- bootstrap produces a graph that looks impressive but does not improve the\n  next agent interaction\n\n## What The Recent Engine Work Enables\n\nThe recent compiler/MCP/CLI work is meaningful because it supports the memory\nloop:\n\n- `compile_ontology` turns markdown frontmatter into a deterministic graph\n  artifact with `graphHash`, issue counts, aliases, pagination, and optional\n  indexes.\n- `query_ontology` lets agents ask graph-database-like questions without\n  pulling the whole vault into context.\n- `health` and `workspace_brief` give first-contact diagnosis before an agent\n  starts editing.\n- `maintenance_plan` turns graph cleanup into an ordered work queue.\n- Structured MCP schemas and error codes let agents recover from bad calls\n  instead of parsing fragile prose.\n- `analyze_repo_structure` and `infer_imports` reduce the cost of creating the\n  first draft from code.\n\nThis is the difference between a markdown graph toy and an agent-usable memory\nworkbench.\n\n## Agent Builder Concerns Lens\n\nUse this lens when deciding whether a new Ontology Atlas feature is worth adding.\nThe point is not to copy any agent framework UI. The point is to answer the\noperational problems that experienced agent builders keep designing around.\n\nCurrent research scan, 2026-06-05:\n\n- Anthropic's agent guidance stresses that simple workflows should stay simple,\n  while true agents should be reserved for open-ended loops that need tools and\n  environmental feedback:\n  <https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents>\n- Claude Code guidance repeatedly treats context as the scarce resource:\n  project memory files, subagents, skills, and task-focused sessions exist to\n  keep the main agent context clean and durable:\n  <https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices>\n  <https://support.claude.com/en/articles/14553240-give-claude-context-claude-md-and-better-prompts>\n  <https://support.claude.com/en/articles/14554000-claude-code-power-user-tips>\n- OpenAI's Codex and Agents SDK guidance puts persistent repo instructions,\n  issue-shaped prompts, MCP connections, approvals, and human-in-the-loop\n  resumption at the center of agent development:\n  <https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/6a2631dc-783e-479b-b1a4-af0cfbd38630/how-openai-uses-codex.pdf>\n  <https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-js/guides/mcp/>\n- Cognition's agent verification writing frames async coding-agent output as\n  incomplete until it includes end-to-end proof artifacts such as labeled\n  screenshots, videos, and pass/fail assertions:\n  <https://cognition.ai/blog/testing-development>\n- Google ADK frames agent infrastructure around structured context, sessions,\n  memory, tools, callbacks, traces, failures, and resumability:\n  <https://adk.dev/>\n- LangChain's memory work separates semantic, episodic, and procedural memory\n  so agents can distinguish durable facts, prior episodes, and reusable skills:\n  <https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/deepagents/memory>\n- MCP frames agent integration as a typed boundary for tools, resources, and\n  prompts rather than an ad hoc pile of API calls:\n  <https://modelcontextprotocol.io/>\n\nTranslate those concerns into Atlas product tests:\n\n1. **Context selection**: Does the feature help an agent get the smallest useful\n   packet instead of flooding the context window?\n2. **Durable memory**: Does it turn an agent discovery into repo-backed markdown\n   that the next Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor session can reuse?\n3. **Tool and relation clarity**: Does it expose which MCP tool, graph query,\n   node slug, relation type, or source file the agent should use next?\n4. **Traceability**: Can a human see what changed, why it changed, and which\n   graph proof or validation command supports it?\n5. **Guardrails**: Does it fail closed before risky writes, stale assumptions,\n   ambiguous relations, or unverified graph rows become committed memory?\n6. **Human-in-the-loop economy**: Does it ask the developer for review at the\n   right point, not on every low-value observation?\n7. **Resumability**: If a session stops, can another agent reconstruct the\n   working state from the vault, git diff, graph health, and saved briefs?\n\nNear-term product implications:\n\n- Prefer \"agent-ready packets\" over long explanations: selected node profile,\n  blast radius, relation preflight, source file evidence, and next command in\n  one copyable bundle.\n- Make MCP connection and graph health visible as runtime state, not hidden in\n  documentation.\n- Treat draft ontology changes as first-class: staged, reviewable, discardable,\n  and backed by validation before they become durable memory.\n- Keep the visual UI quiet by default, but make proof, trace, and handoff\n  available through popovers, collapsible panels, and copy actions.\n- When adding a feature, ask whether it makes the next agent session cheaper,\n  safer, or more accurate. If not, it is probably decorative.\n\n## Positioning Guardrail\n\nUse \"ontology\" in technical documentation and architecture. Use \"repo-native\nmemory layer\" in launch copy and first-contact README text.\n\nThe marketable promise is not that users can draw a better graph. The promise is\nthat their AI coding agent stops starting from zero.\n\nWhen explaining the code side, use this boundary:\n\n```text\nBuilt-in search, grep, language servers, Serena, CodeGraph, and source indexes\nfind code structure.\nOntology Atlas preserves product/system meaning, implementation evidence, and\nthe next verification path for humans and AI agents.\n```\n",

The user's markdown\nfolder — called the **vault** everywhere in this repo — is the single source of\ntruth. Both the MCP server (used by the AI agent) and the CLI (used by the\ndeveloper) read and write that same folder.\n\nThe public app/website brand is **Ontology Atlas**. The macOS app bundle,\nbundle identifier, and DMG assets use the Ontology Atlas identity, while the\nrepo, CLI binary, and MCP package remain under `ontology-atlas`, so product\nnaming does not imply a backend, Firebase data dependency, or package rename.\n\nAtlas answers graph-database questions by computing the answer when asked. It\nnever keeps a second copy of the data. `compile_ontology` reads the frontmatter\n(the `key: value` block at the top of each `.md` file) and builds one graph file\nin memory — the same vault always produces byte-identical output, which is what\n\"deterministic\" means here. `query_ontology` runs its graph operations over that\nfile, and write tools save confirmed changes back into the markdown.\n\n## Surface contract — web and app\n\nDecided 2026-07-27 (`docs/DECISIONS.md`); the working rule agents load is\n`.claude/rules/surfaces.md`.\n\n**One codebase, one build.** The macOS app is not a port. Tauri loads the very\nsame static export into a WebView (`src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` →\n`frontendDist: \"../out\"`). Nothing is forked, and nothing should be.\n\n**What each surface is for.** The app is where the vault lives day to day: the\nplace a person reads the map, judges it, and connects their AI agents. The web\nhas two jobs, in this order. (1) It is the **gateway** — it opens the map with\nno install, for a demo, someone's first five minutes, or a shared link. (2) It\nis a **second-best workbench** where no app exists yet, which in practice means\nChromium on Windows and Linux, because the whole web surface needs the browser's\nFile System Access API to work at all. The order matters: every visitor we have\nobserved so far arrived through the web, so treating the gateway as merely \"the\nWindows stand-in\" would downgrade the only way people currently arrive. A\nWindows app would end job 2 on that operating system; job 1 never expires.\n\n**The two surfaces differ in a small set of modules — we call them bridges — not\nin the router.** Each bridge is the single file that calls a desktop-only\nability. The canonical roster is the bridge table in\n`.claude/rules/surfaces.md`; the ones that shape this document are:\n\n| Bridge | Module | Web behaviour |\n|---|---|---|\n| Vault absolute path | `src/shared/lib/tauri-vault-fs.ts` | FSA handle instead (no path) |\n| Git | `src/shared/lib/tauri-git.ts` | Cannot run → degraded card |\n| Keychain | `src/shared/lib/tauri-secrets.ts` | Impossible by design → degraded card |\n| LLM calls | `src/shared/lib/tauri-llm.ts` | Impossible by design → action not rendered |\n| In-app agent runtime (ACP) | `src/shared/lib/tauri-acp.ts` | A browser cannot spawn a process → one row states why and where |\n\nEvery bridge follows one convention: `getInvoke()` returns `null` when\n`isTauri()` is false, and the screen then says plainly that it cannot do this\nhere. Such a screen must tell the reader two things: **why** it cannot work here\nand **where** it can. New desktop abilities attach the same way. They do not get\na web version added later — that is a decision we have made, not a task waiting\nin a backlog.\n\n**What \"the same data\" actually guarantees.** The folder on disk is the single\nsource of truth, and both surfaces open the *physically same folder* — the web\nthrough an FSA handle, the app through an absolute path. The two surfaces cannot\nend up reading the same file differently, because one contract test runs the\nsame file through all three parsers (web, MCP, scripts) and the schema is\nwritten in exactly one place, `mcp/src/schema.mjs`. Anything that has to pass\nbetween the two surfaces is written **inside the vault** as plain text —\n`.ontology-atlas/activity.jsonl` and `llm-audit.jsonl`. That is the rule this\ncontract sets: *anything shared between surfaces lives in the vault folder.* If\ntwo people (or an agent and a person) edit at once,\n`patch_concept(expected_mtime)` stops the second write from silently erasing the\nfirst.\n\nThree things are deliberately **not** shared. The \"last opened vault\" handle:\neach surface keeps its own IndexedDB, so you pick the folder once per surface,\nand `/download`'s install step 02 says so. API keys: they live in the operating\nsystem's credential store, app only. UI preferences: localStorage. API keys and\npersonal display settings are not part of the shared model, so they stay out of\nthe vault.\n\n**Verification is split three ways** (this replaced the old web↔app round-trip\ncheck, which the same decision abolished). First, screens that both surfaces\nshare are tested once in the browser, and that counts as proof for the app too,\nbecause it is literally the same bundle — the exceptions are font rendering,\nscrolling, and the window frame, which still have to be checked in the installed\napp. Second, desktop-only abilities are proved *only* in the installed app.\nThird, the web surface has its own test, `tests/e2e/web-surface-smoke.spec.ts`,\nwired into `.github/workflows/e2e.yml` on a deliberately wider condition than\nthe rest of the suite — nobody watches the web by hand, so that test is its only\nguard.\n\nA deep link built on one surface is not guaranteed to rebuild the same screen on\nthe other. Where such links work, treat it as a convenience, not a promise.\n\n### In-app coding agents over ACP (2026-08-16)\n\nThe app can launch a coding agent the user already installed (Claude Code,\nCodex, …) and speak the Agent Client Protocol v1 to it over stdio. It is a\ndesktop-only ability, so it attaches through the bridge convention above. Both\ndecision records behind it are in `docs/DECISIONS.md`, dated 2026-08-16.\n\n| Module | Owns |\n|---|---|\n| `src-tauri/src/acp.rs` | Registry snapshot parsing, runtime detection (`ready` · `cli-missing` · `node-missing` · `uvx-missing` · `binary-missing`), launch resolution, isolated config + credential symlink, permission verdict, process-group termination |\n| `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` | The five Tauri commands (`acp_detect_runtimes`, `acp_start`, `acp_send`, `acp_stop`, `acp_permission_verdict`), the four `acp://*` events, and the vault-root rejection that also guards a session's working directory |\n| `src-tauri/src/acp-registry.json` | The committed registry snapshot, generated by `scripts/build-acp-registry.mjs` (`pnpm acp:registry`); icons are fetched at build time into `public/acp-icons/` |\n| `src/shared/lib/tauri-acp.ts` | The capability bridge and its web degradation. It never re-implements the permission policy |\n| `src/features/acp-session/` | `acp-client.ts` (JSON-RPC framing over the bridge) and `use-acp-session.ts` (one session's lifetime; a permission request blocks until the screen answers, and an unanswered one is refused) |\n| `src/widgets/acp-chat-panel/` | `AcpChatPanel.tsx` and `AcpPermissionCard.tsx`. **No route or control opens this panel yet**, so it is a module, not a surface |\n| `src/widgets/app-settings-menu/ui/AcpRuntimeSettings.tsx` | The one ACP surface a user can reach today: the settings sheet's Runtime section |\n\nThree properties are structural rather than incidental. The registry is a\ncommitted snapshot, so no ACP code path touches the network at runtime. A\nlaunched session runs against an app-owned config directory rather than the\nuser's global one, which is what makes the permission gate exist at all;\ncredentials are symlinked, never copied. And the child runs in its own process\ngroup, so quitting the app ends the adapter and everything it spawned.\n\n## FSD layers\n\n```\nsrc/\n├── app/        providers, init code\n├── views/      page-level components (1 view per route or group)\n├── widgets/    composite UI blocks (topology-map-v2, drawer, palette, …)\n├── features/   single interaction units (form, picker, search, …)\n├── entities/   business entities (project, ontology-class, …)\n└── shared/     UI primitives, lib helpers, config, types\n```\n\n**Import direction**: `app → views → widgets → features → entities → shared`. ESLint blocks the reverse.\n\nThe directory layout is enforced by `eslint-plugin-boundaries` in `eslint.config.mjs`.\n\n## Data flow\n\n### Vault graph lifecycle\n\n1. Markdown files are loaded from the vault folder.\n2. Each file's frontmatter becomes one **node** (a thing in the model: a\n   project, a domain, a capability, or an element), and the lists inside that\n   frontmatter become **relations** (typed links between two nodes).\n3. `compile_ontology` puts those nodes and relations into one fixed order,\n   resolves aliases, collects the problems it found, lists the frontmatter\n   arrays it would tidy up, computes a stable `graphHash` (one fingerprint for\n   the meaning of the whole graph), and optionally builds query indexes.\n4. `query_ontology` serves graph operations such as `neighbors`, `path`,\n   `project_scope`, `blast_radius`, `cycles`, `maintenance_plan`,\n   `workspace_brief`, and `health`. `agent_brief` accepts an explicit project\n   in multi-project vaults and derives a fresh categorical `meaningAssessment`\n   for that project; it does not reuse a saved score.\n5. Write tools change the markdown only when someone explicitly calls\n   add/patch/relation/rename/merge/delete. Analysis tools such as\n   `analyze_repo_structure` and `infer_imports` only propose candidates; they\n   never change a file.\n6. `finalize_project_meaning` runs after the writes are done. Once the vault\n   validates and the project's scope is complete, it saves a versioned record —\n   a *receipt* — of the competency answers, tied to the current graph and to\n   where the source code came from. Writing that receipt successfully does\n   **not** mean the source is still current. Three things stay separate and are\n   never added up into one number: whether the structure is complete, whether\n   each competency answer has evidence behind it, and whether the source has\n   been rechecked. If evidence is missing, out of date, or unresolved, the\n   assessment closes in one of the named review/evidence states instead of a\n   score.\n\n### Dual node identity\n\nEvery node carries two names. The `uid` is a lowercase UUIDv4 that never\nchanges — it is the node's permanent identity. The `slug` is the short readable\nname in the file path and the URL, and it can be renamed at any time. The\ncompiler requires UID validity and uniqueness,\nincludes UID and merge-owned `merged_uids` in the semantic hash, and exposes\n`uidToSlug`, `slugToUid`, and `mergedUidToSlug`. Compiled nodes and agent-facing\nnode summaries carry both values. Relations, adjacency maps, edge endpoints,\nURLs, and canvas IDs remain slug-based; exact MCP identity lookup and interop\nexports use UID. JSON-LD/GraphML serialize identity as `urn:uuid:<uid>`.\nRename/reclassify preserve UID; merge preserves the survivor and absorbs source\nidentity history. Invalid identity aborts compilation rather than producing a\npartial artifact.\n\n### Vault mode (user picked a markdown folder)\n\n1. `useLocalVault()` returns `{ status: 'loaded', handle, manifest }`.\n2. `useDataSourceMode()` returns `'local'`.\n3. `useProjects()` derives projects from `manifest.docs` (filter `kind: project`).\n4. `useOntologyInsight()` derives ontology nodes/edges from vault frontmatter.\n5. Mutations (`useProjectMutations.updateProject`) write directly to `.md`\n   files — the installed desktop app uses the Tauri native vault bridge,\n   hosted web uses the browser File System Access API directly (root-first-open,\n   2026-07: `FirstRunStarterModule` in the topology hub's INDEX panel calls\n   `useLocalVault().open()` on `/` itself, no install required). `/docs`'s own\n   separate local-source tab (browsing a second vault as a documentation\n   source inside that page) stays desktop-gated — that is a narrower, older\n   feature unrelated to opening your primary vault from the map.\n6. Ontology block import/export follows the same split: browser\n   `showDirectoryPicker()` or the Tauri `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` shim feeds\n   one recursive block reader/writer. Import plans conflicts before writing;\n   native or browser picker cancellation has no side effect.\n\nThe MCP server is independent: it reads the same vault directory through the\nfilesystem (Node.js `fs`), not the WebView bridge. AI agents and the installed app end up with the same view.\n\n### Static mode (no vault picked)\n\n1. `useLocalVault()` returns `{ status: 'closed' }`.\n2. `useDataSourceMode()` returns `'static'`.\n3. `useProjects()` derives projects from the build-time dogfood manifest\n   (`docs/ontology/` compiled into `src/entities/docs-vault/data/manifest.json`\n   by `pnpm docs-vault:build`).\n4. Mutations are blocked with a toast pointing to the vault picker.\n\nThis is what a first-time visitor sees: a real, filled-in ontology — this\nproject's own — before they have picked any folder of their own.\n\n## Routes\n\n```\n/                          shows a different screen depending on who opens it. A web visitor\n                           with no vault gets the gateway — the install-free intro page, the\n                           same view /download renders. A web user who has a vault, and anyone\n                           in the installed app, gets the map (or first run) unchanged. The\n                           installed app must never offer \"download this app\" to someone who is\n                           already running it. One function decides: isGatewaySurface() in\n                           shared/lib/nav-destination\n/topology                  map + contextual relation editor + change review. Any link labelled\n                           \"map\" or \"edit relation\" points here, not at / (gate:\n                           tests/contract/map-destination-route.contract.test.ts)\n/docs                      vault picker / editor / unified palette\n/ontology                  thin redirect → /topology?index=expanded (old tree/ego hub retired, B3)\n/ontology/edit             compatibility redirect → /topology contextual workbench\n/ontology/studio           compatibility redirect; translates node/mode/edit/via/review to /topology\n/ontology/insights         five-question maintenance board\n/git                       local vault git history / snapshot workbench (desktop-only destination)\n/agents                    coding agents this computer can run — the app launches them and\n                           you talk to them here. Fetches Node and the pinned CLI into an\n                           app-only folder when they are missing, runs the eight-step\n                           connection check, and repairs what it can. Moved out of the\n                           settings sheet 2026-08-20 (ledger 90): settings is where you pick\n                           values, this is operational work with progress state. API Key and\n                           Workspace stay in settings (the 2026-08-16 freeze and a different\n                           owning domain). Desktop launches the tools; on the web the page\n                           still renders and says what it cannot do, plus what it can.\n/projects                  project list (cards)\n/project/[slug]            project detail (inline edit when vault loaded)\n/project/[slug]/edit       full project editor\n/project/new               new project form\n/project/fallback          fallback page for missing slugs\n/download                  the same gateway page at its own address — it keeps the breadcrumb\n                           and the back-to-map link that / leaves out\n/guide                     the project guide, several chapters rendering docs/guide/*.md vault\n                           docs. Named `guide` and not `docs` because /docs is already the\n                           vault workbench (2026-07-30 ledger). Order and slugs live once in\n                           src/views/gateway-doc/model/guide-pages.ts\n/guide/[segment]           one chapter; static params come from that same registry\n/changelog                 renders docs/CHANGELOG.md from the vault, most recent sections only\n```\n\nAll routes are wrapped under `/[locale]/` by next-intl (en, ko).\n\n> Removed in earlier rounds: `/admin/*`, `/review/*`, `/diagnostics/*`,\n> `/knowledge/*`. Removed in Round 10: `/login`, `/signup`, `/account`,\n> `/reset-password`, `/settings/*`.\n\n**One piece of code decides which nav item is active; each screen size shows a\ndifferent list of buttons.** The desktop rail shows six destinations: Map, Docs,\nInsights, Projects, Agents, and Git. The mobile bottom bar shows four: Map,\nDocs, Insights, and Projects — contextual writing stays inside Map, while Agents\nand Git are desktop-only. Both read the same rules in\n`src/shared/lib/nav-destination.ts`, so every route belongs to exactly one\ndestination even on a screen size that deliberately hides that button. The\nretired\n`OperationsNav` and `OntologySubNav` are deleted. `AppSettingsMenu` mounts\nthrough the shell, while `AgentActivityChip` mounts in Topology's contextual map\ncontrols and `AgentActivitySettings` configures that current-work flow; none is\na navigation destination.\n\n## URL contract (query-param + node id grammar)\n\nA URL is how this app shares a view. A copied link, a handoff to an AI agent,\nand the browser's back button must all rebuild the same screen, so one node has\nto be written the same way everywhere. The **standard way to write a node id is\n`<kind>:<slug>`** — the kind in singular, a colon, then the slug, for example\n`capability:mcp-server`, `domain:views`, `project:ontology-atlas`. That is the\nvalue carried in `?node=` / `?p=` / `?realm=` and the id used in the text handed\nto an agent.\n\n### Query params\n\n| Param | Screen(s) | Meaning | Value shape |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| `p` | `/`, `/topology` | focused/selected node | canonical `<kind>:<slug>` (bare slug tolerated) |\n| `open` | `/`, `/topology` | density-gate expanded parents | comma list of node ids |\n| `realm` | `/`, `/topology` | \"realm\" containment-subtree root | canonical `<kind>:<slug>` (bare slug promoted) |\n| `mode` | `/`, `/topology` | analysis mode | `overview` \\| `focus` \\| `path` \\| `health` |\n| `pathFrom` / `pathTo` (aliases `from` / `to`) | `/`, `/topology` | path source / target | node id |\n| `hub` · `c` · `impact` · `pulse` · `index` · `create` | `/`, `/topology` | focused hub · category · impact mode · pulse window · INDEX panel state · legacy create-node intent | see `src/views/home/model/url-state.ts` |\n| `workbench` · `edit` | `/topology` | contextual writer state · optional relation/target | `edit` \\| `create`; `edit=<relation>:<targetId>` |\n| `recent` · `ask` | `/`, `/topology` | recent-change lens · agent first-words intent | typed parsers in `src/views/home/model/url-state.ts` |\n| `via` | `/`, `/topology`, `/ontology` | origin marker for the return chip | `insights:<tab>` |\n| `review` | `/`, `/topology`, `/ontology/insights` | exact Do-next review row carried across handoff | stable review id, only meaningful with the matching handoff |\n| `node` | `/ontology` (redirect) | node to focus after redirect → `?p=` | node id (translated by `translateOntologyDeeplinkToTopologyParam`) |\n| `node` · `mode` · `edit` | `/ontology/edit`, `/ontology/studio` | legacy write deep link translated to `p/workbench/edit` | canonical and plural-folder node forms tolerated; `mode=create` → `workbench=create` |\n| `slug` | `/docs` | vault file to open | vault file path (`ontology/capabilities/foo`), not a node id — file paths are the docs vault's own address space |\n| `tab` | `/ontology/insights` | active maintenance question | `do-next` \\| `composition` \\| `connections` \\| `boundaries` \\| `freshness` |\n\n### One place builds node ids, one place reads them\n\n- **Building a link** (map · Insights · popovers · datasheets, all staying in\n  the map workbench): `buildTopologyMeaningEditorNodeHref` and\n  `buildTopologyMeaningEditorEdgeHref` in\n  `src/entities/knowledge-graph/lib/ontology-node-href.ts`. It normalizes any\n  input to canonical via `translateOntologyDeeplinkToTopologyParam`\n  (`capabilities/foo` → `capability:foo`; already-canonical / bare /\n  evidence-path pass through).\n- **Compatibility redirect**: `OntologyEditRedirectPage` normalizes legacy\n  `/ontology/edit` and `/ontology/studio` query values and replaces the route\n  with `/topology?p=...&workbench=edit` or `/topology?workbench=create`.\n- **Reading a link**: `parseHomeRouteState` owns `p/workbench/edit`, and\n  `parseOntologyStudioEditParam` remains only as the compatibility parser for\n  the relation/target pair. Unknown relation values open no editor rather than guessing.\n- **Turning a node id into a docs file path** happens in exactly one place: the\n  popover and the datasheet pass the focus model's `sourceSlug` (a path to a\n  file in the vault) straight into `buildDocsVaultHref({ slug })`. `/docs`\n  addresses files, not nodes, so its `?slug=` deliberately stays a file path.\n- Both the building and the reading side are pinned by `ontology-node-href.test.ts`,\n  `translate-ontology-deeplink.test.ts`, and\n  `url-state.test.ts`, and `OntologyEditRedirectPage.test.tsx`. Insights tab\n  serialization is separately pinned by `insights-tab-state.test.ts`.\n\n## Build pipeline\n\n```bash\npnpm docs-vault:build      # docs/ontology/*.md → src/entities/docs-vault/data/manifest.json\npnpm docs-vault:check      # verify committed docs-vault outputs are fresh\npnpm build                 # next build → static export → out/\npnpm vault:validate        # R11+ — frontmatter integrity + graph array drift\npnpm test:vault:validate   # focused validator CLI argument contract\npnpm vault:audit           # dogfood ontology capability/element paths exist in repo\npnpm test:vault:audit      # focused vault audit CLI argument contract\npnpm package:check         # MCP/CLI package files contract + self-test\npnpm test:contracts        # focused cross-package parser/schema/validator contracts\npnpm vault:migrate --list  # R11 — schema migration runner (dry-run default)\n```\n\nThe `docs-vault:build` step is automatic via `predev` and `prebuild` npm hooks. `docs-vault:check` also runs in the macOS release workflow, while `vault:validate`, `test:vault:validate`, `vault:audit`, `test:vault:audit`, and `package:check` remain explicit local/release-preflight gates.\n\n## i18n routing contract\n\n- In-app navigation: `Link`, `useRouter`, `usePathname` from `@/i18n/navigation` (locale-aware).\n- Locale-agnostic browser history: `useSearchParams` from `next/navigation` (no locale concern).\n- Cross-locale pivot (LocaleSwitch): raw `next/navigation` router, intentional.\n- Translation key namespaces: see `messages/{en,ko}.json` (kept in parity).\n\n## Test surface\n\n- **Vitest** (`tests/` + co-located `*.test.ts`) — unit + component\n- **Playwright** (`tests/e2e/*.spec.ts`) — visual regression, a11y, navigation, mobile\n\nE2E tests no longer rely on Firebase emulators (R10b removed firebase-tools\nand emulator scripts entirely).\n\n## Source-of-truth files\n\nWhen docs and code disagree, code wins:\n\n- `package.json` — what's installed, what scripts exist\n- `next.config.ts` — output mode, image config\n- `app/layout.tsx` — metadata, providers, head\n- `eslint.config.mjs` — FSD boundary rules\n\nLong-form docs:\n\n- [`AGENTS.md`](../AGENTS.md) — contributor guide (canonical for AI tools)\n- [`PRODUCT-DIRECTION.md`](./PRODUCT-DIRECTION.md) — mission\n- [`FEATURES.md`](./FEATURES.md) — currently shipping features\n- [`DESIGN-SYSTEM.md`](./DESIGN-SYSTEM.md) — design tokens + forbidden patterns\n- [`DEPLOYMENT.md`](./DEPLOYMENT.md) — static export → static hosting\n- [`CHANGELOG.md`](./CHANGELOG.md) — chronological user-visible changes\n- [`docs/archive/`](./archive/) — historical analysis docs (earlier cloud-mode designs, retired surfaces)\n",
